the evidence for diversity as a causal defense is weaker than it appears. The observed ASR reduction conflates panel diversity (judge independence) with individual robustness (strongest members). No existing work disentangles these across panel sizes. BFT adversaries control faulty nodes internally, whereas evaluation adversaries manipulate inputs externally; the SDPI bounds joint failure but assumes non-adaptive faults \\citep{schneier2000secrets}.\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/introduction.tex-13-\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/introduction.tex:14:[Omitted long matching line]\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/introduction.tex-15-\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/introduction.tex-16-[Omitted long context line]\n--\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/introduction.tex-18-These findings yield three contributions:\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/introduction.tex-19-\\begin{enumerate}\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/introduction.tex:20:[Omitted long matching line]\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/introduction.tex:21:\\item \\textbf{Attack-type taxonomy.} GAM reveals a 6:6 split by attack mechanism at $K{=}3$ (targeted vs.\\ shared-bias), 11/12 consistent at $K{=}5$. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient positive at aggregate level but direction depends on attack mechanism (\\S\\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/introduction.tex-22-\\item \\textbf{Practical defense recommendations.} Robustness-first selection ($\\min \\eta_{\\max}$) dominates diversity-maximizing strategies. <details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01NnUj6p1r6MjDE65MJJTJEa`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	
3	\begin{figure*}[t]
4	\centering
5	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/fig_framework.pdf}
6	\caption{Overview of our evaluation framework. Given $N$ candidate LLM judges, we construct $K$-judge panels (\S\ref{sec:experiments}), subject them to six adversarial attack types (\S\ref{sec:threat_model}), and assess vulnerability through two complementary metrics: $\eta_{\max}$ (weakest-link individual susceptibility) and $K_{\text{eff}}$ (effective independent channels via mutual information). Panel decisions are aggregated through four methods of increasing sophistication (\S\ref{sec:aggregation}).}
7	\label{fig:framework}
8	\end{figure*}
9	
10	Multi-judge panels have emerged as the dominant defense against bias and manipulation in LLM-based evaluation. After \citet{zheng2023judging} demonstrated that individual LLM judges can rival human annotators, subsequent work exposed systematic vulnerabilities: position bias, verbosity preferences, and sycophancy compromise the reliability of any single judge \citep{wang2023fair}. The natural response has been to aggregate decisions across diverse models, drawing inspiration from Byzantine fault tolerance (BFT), where majority voting among independent nodes tolerates faulty responses \citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical}. A recent systematization of LLM-judge security reports that 7-model committees reduce attack success rates from 50--68\% to 10--19\% \citep{almasoud2026security}. Diversity, by this logic, is the key ingredient for secure evaluation.
11	
12	Yet the evidence for diversity as a causal defense is weaker than it appears. The observed ASR reduction conflates panel diversity (judge independence) with individual robustness (strongest members). No existing work disentangles these across panel sizes. BFT adversaries control faulty nodes internally, whereas evaluation adversaries manipulate inputs externally; the SDPI bounds joint failure but assumes non-adaptive faults \citep{schneier2000secrets}.
13	
14	In our experiments, we observe a panel-size-dependent vulnerability regime transition. At $K{=}3$, $\eta_{\max}$ dominates; at $K{\geq}5$, this reverses ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ dominant, $\eta_{\max}$ non-significant). At the aggregate level, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is positively associated with vulnerability in both regimes, consistent with one possible mechanism where independent judges present more targetable attack surfaces \citep{goodfellow2015adversarial,papernot2016transferability}. However, this pattern is attack-mechanism-dependent: GAM analysis shows higher $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ associated with \emph{increased} vulnerability for targeted attacks but \emph{decreased} vulnerability for shared-bias attacks, and Bayesian calibration corresponds to a sign change.
15	
16	Controlled experiments across 15 models, $K \in \{3,5,7\}$, 6 attacks, and 2 benchmarks confirm this transition (Figure~\ref{fig:framework}). Under random faults, panel composition is highly predictive ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$); the predictability collapse under adaptive attack is most pronounced at $K{=}3$. Bayesian calibration corresponds to a sign change in $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction at $K{=}3$ and renders both predictors negligible at $K{=}5$; robustness-first selection dominates diversity-maximizing strategies.
17	
18	These findings yield three contributions:
19	\begin{enumerate}
20	\item \textbf{K-dependent regime transition.} While CJT predicts that panel size affects aggregation quality, we quantify a specific transition: $\eta_{\max}$ dominates at $K{=}3$ (bootstrap 5/12, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 1/12, MDE${=}0.14$); at $K{\geq}5$, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the primary predictor (7/12, $\eta_{\max}$ 0/12). Beyond this aggregate pattern, we document attack-mechanism-dependent structure (6:6 targeted/shared-bias split) and aggregation modulation (Bayesian reversal), neither predicted by CJT (\S\ref{sec:dominance}, \S\ref{sec:cross_k}, \S\ref{sec:rf_contrast}).
21	\item \textbf{Attack-type taxonomy.} GAM reveals a 6:6 split by attack mechanism at $K{=}3$ (targeted vs.\ shared-bias), 11/12 consistent at $K{=}5$. $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient positive at aggregate level but direction depends on attack mechanism (\S\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).
22	\item \textbf{Practical defense recommendations.} Robustness-first selection ($\min \eta_{\max}$) dominates diversity-maximizing strategies. Bayesian calibration corresponds to a sign change at $K{=}3$ and renders both predictors negligible at $K{=}5$ (\S\ref{sec:aggregation}, \S\ref{sec:analysis}).
23	\end{enumerate}
24

BFT adversaries control faulty nodes internally, whereas evaluation adversaries manipulate inputs externally; the SDPI bounds joint failure but assumes non-adaptive faults \\citep{schneier2000secrets}.\n\nIn our experiments, we observe a panel-size-dependent vulnerability regime transition. At $K{=}3$, $\\eta_{\\max}$ dominates; at $K{\\geq}5$, this reverses ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ dominant, $\\eta_{\\max}$ non-significant). At the aggregate level, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is positively associated with vulnerability in both regimes, consistent with one possible mechanism where independent judges present more targetable attack surfaces \\citep{goodfellow2015adversarial,papernot2016transferability}. However, this pattern is attack-mechanism-dependent: GAM analysis shows higher $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ associated with \\emph{increased} vulnerability for targeted attacks but \\emph{decreased} vulnerability for shared-bias attacks, and Bayesian calibration corresponds to a sign change.\n\nControlled experiments across 15 models, $K \\in \\{3,5,7\\}$, 6 attacks, and 2 benchmarks confirm this transition (Figure~\\ref{fig:framework}). Under random faults, panel composition is highly predictive ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$); the predictability collapse under adaptive attack is most pronounced at $K{=}3$. Bayesian calibration corresponds to a sign change in $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction at $K{=}3$ and renders both predictors negligible at $K{=}5$; robustness-first selection dominates diversity-maximizing strategies.\n\nThese findings yield three contributions:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n\\item \\textbf{K-dependent regime transition.} While CJT predicts that panel size affects aggregation quality, we quantify a specific transition: $\\eta_{\\max}$ dominates at $K{=}3$ (bootstrap 5/12, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 1/12, MDE${=}0.14$); at $K{\\geq}5$, $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ becomes the primary predictor (7/12, $\\eta_{\\max}$ 0/12). Beyond this aggregate pattern, we document attack-mechanism-dependent structure (6:6 targeted/shared-bias split) and aggregation modulation (Bayesian reversal), neither predicted by CJT (\\S\\ref{sec:dominance}, \\S\\ref{sec:cross_k}, \\S\\ref{sec:rf_contrast}).\n\\item \\textbf{Attack-type taxonomy.} GAM reveals a 6:6 split by attack mechanism at $K{=}3$ (targeted vs.\\ shared-bias), 11/12 consistent at $K{=}5$. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient positive at aggregate level but direction depends on attack mechanism (\\S\\ref{sec:gam_taxonomy}).\n\\item \\textbf{Practical defense recommendations.} Robustness-first selection ($\\min \\eta_{\\max}$) dominates diversity-maximizing strategies. Bayesian calibration corresponds to a sign change at $K{=}3$ and renders both predictors negligible at $K{=}5$ (\\S\\ref{sec:aggregation}, \\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
      "numLines": 24,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 24
    }

1	\section{Analysis}
2	\label{sec:analysis}
3	
4	\paragraph{Regime Transition Mechanism.}
5	At $K{=}3$, a majority requires $t{=}2$; a single compromised judge flips the panel, making $\eta_{\max}$ the binding constraint. At $K{\geq}5$, the adversary must compromise ${\geq}3$ judges, and high-$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ panels present more distinct attack surfaces. The transition threshold $K^*$ lies between 3 and 5.
6	
7	\paragraph{Attack-Mechanism-Dependent Structure.}
8	The aggregate positive $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient masks a 6:6 directional split at $K{=}3$ (11/12 consistent at $K{=}5$; Appendix~\ref{app:bft_reversal}). Targeted attacks are consistent with judge-specific vulnerabilities amplified by independence; shared-bias attacks are consistent with common biases diluted by diversity. Unconditional claims about diversity being ``harmful'' or ``beneficial'' are thus misleading.
9	
10	\paragraph{Diversity--Capability Confound.}
11	$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ are correlated at $r{=}0.70$ ($K{=}3$) and $r{=}0.81$ ($K{=}5$; Figure~\ref{fig:keff_eta_scatter}), reflecting a structural property: maximizing diversity requires sampling across capability tiers, introducing weaker judges (Appendix~\ref{app:diversity_capability}). Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis \citep{cinelli2020making} shows the known $\eta_{\max}$ confound exceeds $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s robustness value in 28/36 conditions (Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity}), with robustness increasing with $K$ (1/12 $\to$ 3/12 $\to$ 4/12). The API-only subset ($r{=}0.85$) does not replicate the transition (3/12). Capability-matched subsampling ($r{=}0.50$, 9 models, 126 panels at $K{=}5$; Figure~\ref{fig:keff_eta_scatter}, orange) provides partial mitigation: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ strengthens to 9/12 and RV exceeds $\eta_{\max}$ in 6/12 conditions (conservatively 5/12; Appendix~\ref{app:sensitivity_poolA}), versus 3/12 in the full pool. The transition is thus a descriptive regime shift whose causal attribution to diversity requires capability-controlled designs.
12	
13	\begin{figure}[t]
14	\centering
15	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_keff_eta_scatter.pdf}
16	\caption{Panel-level correlation between $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ at $K{=}5$ ($n{=}3{,}003$ panels). Full model pool (gray, $r{=}0.81$) exhibits strong collinearity; capability-matched Pool~A (orange diamonds, $r{=}0.50$, 126 panels) shows reduced confounding.}
17	\label{fig:keff_eta_scatter}
18	\end{figure}
19	
20	\paragraph{Aggregation and Deployment.}
21	Bayesian calibration is associated with a reversal of $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s direction at $K{=}3$ (6/7 negative; Table~\ref{tab:aggregation}) and renders both predictors negligible at $K{=}5$ (Table~\ref{tab:aggregation_k5}). Robustness-first selection ($\min \eta_{\max}$) achieves lowest ASR in 12/12 conditions (Appendix~\ref{app:panel_selection}). Recomputing MI under attack confirms stability ($\rho{\geq}0.91$ for 4/6 attacks; position bias disrupts MI on ARC, $\rho{=}0.08$; Appendix~\ref{app:attacked_keff}). The random-fault contrast (\S\ref{sec:rf_contrast}) confirms $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ predicts under non-adaptive noise ($R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$), with the predictability collapse most pronounced at $K{=}3$.
22

1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	\subsection{Weakest-Link Dominance at \texorpdfstring{$K{=}3$}{K=3}}
5	\label{sec:dominance}
6	
7	Table~\ref{tab:dominance} reports the log-log OLS regression (Equation~\ref{eq:regression}) for all 12 conditions across the 455 panels ($K{=}3$, 15 judges from Table~\ref{tab:models}).
8	
9	\begin{table}[t]
10	\centering
11	\small
12	\begin{tabular}{llccc}
13	\toprule
14	\textbf{Bench.} & \textbf{Attack} & $\boldsymbol{\eta_{\max}}$ & $\boldsymbol{K_{\mathrm{eff}}}$ & \textbf{Dom.} \\
15	\midrule
16	MMLU & Prompt inj.    & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\
17	MMLU & Sycophancy     & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\
18	MMLU & Score manip.   & n.s.    & n.s.    & -- \\
19	MMLU & Verbosity      & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\
20	MMLU & Position bias  & $+^{*}$ & n.s.    & $\eta$ \\
21	MMLU & Authority bias & $+^{*}$ & n.s.    & $\eta$ \\
22	\addlinespace
23	ARC  & Prompt inj.    & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\
24	ARC  & Sycophancy     & $+^{*}$ & $-^{*}$ & Co \\
25	ARC  & Score manip.   & n.s.    & $+^{*}$ & $K$ \\
26	ARC  & Verbosity      & $+^{*}$ & n.s.    & $\eta$ \\
27	ARC  & Position bias  & n.s.    & $+^{*}$ & $K$ \\
28	ARC  & Authority bias & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\
29	\bottomrule
30	\end{tabular}
31	\caption{Standardized regression coefficients for $\eta_{\max}$ and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ across 12 conditions (455 panels, log-log OLS). $+^{*}$/$-^{*}$: FDR-significant positive/negative (Benjamini--Hochberg, $q < 0.05$); n.s.: not significant. Dom.\ column: $\eta$ = $\eta_{\max}$ sole dominant, $K$ = $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ sole dominant, Co = co-dominant \citep{budescu1993dominance}.}
32	\label{tab:dominance}
33	\end{table}
34	
35	$\eta_{\max}$ reaches FDR significance in 9/12 conditions (exceptions: MMLU $\times$ score manipulation, ARC $\times$ score manipulation, and ARC $\times$ position bias), consistent with Theorem~\ref{thm:monotonicity}. $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is significant positive in 7/12 and negative in 1 (ARC $\times$ sycophancy). Dominance analysis yields 3 $\eta_{\max}$-dominant, 6 co-dominant, 2 $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant, and 1 condition where neither predictor reaches significance. However, the $r = 0.70$ $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$--$\eta_{\max}$ correlation raises confound concerns, and OLS ignores the non-independence of the 455 panels.
36	
37	Each judge appears in $\binom{14}{2} = 91$ of the 455 panels, and each pair co-occurs in 13. This shared structure causes OLS to underestimate standard errors by approximately $2.5{\times}$. Figure~\ref{fig:robustness} compares OLS against three corrections: $\eta_{\max}$ remains robust (8/12 under clustered SE, 5/12 under wild cluster bootstrap), while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ collapses (0/12 and 1/12 respectively). Point estimates remain positive, but confidence intervals contain zero in 11/12 conditions (Appendix~\ref{app:forest_plot}).
38	
39	\begin{figure}[t]
40	\centering
41	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_robustness_comparison.pdf}
42	\caption{Significance counts (FDR $< 0.05$) for $\eta_{\max}$ and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ across four correction methods over 12 experimental conditions ($K{=}3$). Wild cluster bootstrap and clustered standard errors are recommended for the 15-judge combinatorial design.}
43	\label{fig:robustness}
44	\end{figure}
45	
46	Power analysis yields MDE $\beta_{\mathrm{std}} = 0.14$ at 80\% power (MDE decreases to 0.048 at $K{=}5$ and 0.036 at $K{=}7$; Appendix~\ref{app:power_analysis}). $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s non-significance at $K{=}3$ is thus an \emph{informative null}: any true effect is smaller than 0.14 SD.
47	
48	\subsection{Panel-Size-Dependent Regime Transition}
49	\label{sec:cross_k}
50	
51	The $K{=}3$ results above show that $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ fails to reach significance under corrected inference. A natural question is whether this negligibility is intrinsic to judge diversity or an artifact of small panel size. We address this by repeating the wild cluster bootstrap analysis at $K \in \{3, 5, 7\}$, constructing all $\binom{15}{K}$ panels for each value and applying the same specification (Equation~\ref{eq:regression}) with $K$-position max-$p$ correction.
52	
53	\begin{figure}[t]
54	\centering
55	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_regime_transition.pdf}
56	\caption{Regime transition in vulnerability predictors across panel sizes. $\eta_{\max}$ significance (orange) declines from 5/12 to 0/12, while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (blue) rises from 1/12 to 7/12 as $K$ increases. Shaded region marks the transition zone $K^* \in (3, 5)$. Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher weights, position-permutation max-$p$, BH FDR $\alpha{=}0.05$.}
57	\label{fig:regime_transition}
58	\end{figure}
59	
60	Figure~\ref{fig:regime_transition} reveals a sharp regime transition between $K{=}3$ and $K{=}5$. At $K{=}3$, $\eta_{\max}$ is the dominant predictor (5/12) and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is negligible (1/12). At $K{=}5$, $\eta_{\max}$ drops to 0/12 while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ rises to 7/12, all positive. Restricting to the four $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$-stable attacks ($\rho{\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\ref{app:attacked_keff}) yields 4/8 at $K{=}5$, confirming that the signal is not driven by measurement-unstable conditions. The attenuation from 7/12 at $K{=}5$ to 5/12 at $K{=}7$ reflects increased conservatism of the 7-position max-$p$ correction (\S\ref{sec:analysis}).
61	
62	\subsection{Random vs.\ Adaptive Fault Contrast}
63	\label{sec:rf_contrast}
64	
65	We simulate non-adversarial noise by randomly flipping judge votes at observed per-judge error rates. At $K{=}3$, random faults yield $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ with $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ significant in 2/2 conditions; under adaptive attack, significance collapses (1/12). At $K{=}5$, random faults yield $R^2 = 0.705$ ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ 2/2 significant); adaptive attack reduces $R^2$ to 0.256 but $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ retains predictive power (7/12). The gap narrows from ${\sim}0.6$ ($K{=}3$) to $0.45$ ($K{=}5$), confirming the contrast is most pronounced in small panels.
66	
67	\begin{figure}[t]
68	\centering
69	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_gam_keff_split.pdf}
70	\caption{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ effect direction split by attack type at $K{=}3$ (GAM with FDR correction). Targeted attacks show positive $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (higher diversity associated with higher vulnerability), while shared-bias attacks show negative $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (higher diversity associated with lower vulnerability). At $K{=}5$, 11/12 conditions retain this pattern.}
71	\label{fig:gam_split}
72	\end{figure}
73	
74	\subsection{Attack-Type Empirical Categorization}
75	\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}
76	
77	GAM analysis reveals structure masked by the significance collapse (Figure~\ref{fig:gam_split}). At $K{=}3$, the 12 conditions split 6:6 by attack mechanism. \textit{Targeted attacks} (prompt injection, score manipulation, position bias) show positive $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ in all 6 conditions; \textit{shared-bias attacks} (sycophancy, verbosity bias, authority bias) show negative $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ in all 6. Position bias is an anomalous case within the targeted category: while it shows positive $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ direction, its $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ stability is low ($\rho{=}0.08$ on ARC; Appendix~\ref{app:attacked_keff}), placing it closer to a measurement-unstable regime than to the other targeted attacks ($\rho{\geq}0.60$). At $K{=}5$, 11/12 conditions maintain the targeted/shared-bias distinction.
78	
79	\subsection{Aggregation Rules Modulate the Diversity Channel}
80	\label{sec:aggregation}
81	
82	Table~\ref{tab:aggregation} reports regression results for four aggregation methods across all 12 conditions.
83	
84	\begin{table}[t]
85	\centering
86	\small
87	\begin{tabular}{lcccc}
88	\toprule
89	\textbf{Method} & \textbf{$\eta_{\max}$ sig} & \textbf{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} & \textbf{$K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ $\beta$ dir.} & \textbf{$R^2$} \\
90	\midrule
91	Threshold       & 12/12 & 11/12 & 10+/1$-$  & 0.89 \\
92	Majority        & 9/12  & 8/12  & 7+/1$-$   & 0.298 \\
93	Weighted        & 9/12  & 9/12  & 9+/0$-$   & 0.284 \\
94	Bayesian        & 9/12  & 7/12  & 1+/6$-$   & 0.104 \\
95	\bottomrule
96	\end{tabular}
97	\caption{Aggregation method comparison ($K{=}3$, 15 models, 455 panels, log-log canonical spec). Significance counts are FDR-corrected across 12 conditions. $R^2$ is the mean across conditions. Methods are ordered by $R^2$ to highlight the vulnerability gradient from threshold (most predictable) to Bayesian (least).}
98	\label{tab:aggregation}
99	\end{table}
100	
101	
102	The methods form a vulnerability gradient from threshold ($R^2{=}0.89$) to Bayesian ($R^2{=}0.104$; Appendix~\ref{app:aggregation_details}). Threshold voting's high $R^2$ is partly structural: unanimity makes panel failure nearly deterministic given the weakest judge. Bayesian calibration is associated with a sign change in the diversity channel: $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ direction shifts from predominantly positive (7+/1$-$) to predominantly negative (1+/6$-$).
103

      "content": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\n\\subsection{Weakest-Link Dominance at \\texorpdfstring{$K{=}3$}{K=3}}\n\\label{sec:dominance}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:dominance} reports the log-log OLS regression (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) for all 12 conditions across the 455 panels ($K{=}3$, 15 judges from Table~\\ref{tab:models}).\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{llccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Bench.} & \\textbf{Attack} & $\\boldsymbol{\\eta_{\\max}}$ & $\\boldsymbol{K_{\\mathrm{eff}}}$ & \\textbf{Dom.} \\\\\n\\midrule\nMMLU & Prompt inj.    & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Sycophancy     & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Score manip.   & n.s.    & n.s.    & -- \\\\\nMMLU & Verbosity      & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nMMLU & Position bias  & $+^{*}$ & n.s.    & $\\eta$ \\\\\nMMLU & Authority bias & $+^{*}$ & n.s.    & $\\eta$ \\\\\n\\addlinespace\nARC  & Prompt inj.    & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nARC  & Sycophancy     & $+^{*}$ & $-^{*}$ & Co \\\\\nARC  & Score manip.   & n.s.    & $+^{*}$ & $K$ \\\\\nARC  & Verbosity      & $+^{*}$ & n.s.    & $\\eta$ \\\\\nARC  & Position bias  & n.s.    & $+^{*}$ & $K$ \\\\\nARC  & Authority bias & $+^{*}$ & $+^{*}$ & Co \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Standardized regression coefficients for $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across 12 conditions (455 panels, log-log OLS). $+^{*}$/$-^{*}$: FDR-significant positive/negative (Benjamini--Hochberg, $q < 0.05$); n.s.: not significant. Dom.\\ column: $\\eta$ = $\\eta_{\\max}$ sole dominant, $K$ = $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sole dominant, Co = co-dominant \\citep{budescu1993dominance}.}\n\\label{tab:dominance}\n\\end{table}\n\n$\\eta_{\\max}$ reaches FDR significance in 9/12 conditions (exceptions: MMLU $\\times$ score manipulation, ARC $\\times$ score manipulation, and ARC $\\times$ position bias), consistent with Theorem~\\ref{thm:monotonicity}. $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is significant positive in 7/12 and negative in 1 (ARC $\\times$ sycophancy). Dominance analysis yields 3 $\\eta_{\\max}$-dominant, 6 co-dominant, 2 $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-dominant, and 1 condition where neither predictor reaches significance. However, the $r = 0.70$ $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$--$\\eta_{\\max}$ correlation raises confound concerns, and OLS ignores the non-independence of the 455 panels.\n\nEach judge appears in $\\binom{14}{2} = 91$ of the 455 panels, and each pair co-occurs in 13. This shared structure causes OLS to underestimate standard errors by approximately $2.5{\\times}$. Figure~\\ref{fig:robustness} compares OLS against three corrections: $\\eta_{\\max}$ remains robust (8/12 under clustered SE, 5/12 under wild cluster bootstrap), while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ collapses (0/12 and 1/12 respectively). Point estimates remain positive, but confidence intervals contain zero in 11/12 conditions (Appendix~\\ref{app:forest_plot}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_robustness_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{Significance counts (FDR $< 0.05$) for $\\eta_{\\max}$ and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ across four correction methods over 12 experimental conditions ($K{=}3$). Wild cluster bootstrap and clustered standard errors are recommended for the 15-judge combinatorial design.}\n\\label{fig:robustness}\n\\end{figure}\n\nPower analysis yields MDE $\\beta_{\\mathrm{std}} = 0.14$ at 80\\% power (MDE decreases to 0.048 at $K{=}5$ and 0.036 at $K{=}7$; Appendix~\\ref{app:power_analysis}). $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$'s non-significance at $K{=}3$ is thus an \\emph{informative null}: any true effect is smaller than 0.14 SD.\n\n\\subsection{Panel-Size-Dependent Regime Transition}\n\\label{sec:cross_k}\n\nThe $K{=}3$ results above show that $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ fails to reach significance under corrected inference. A natural question is whether this negligibility is intrinsic to judge diversity or an artifact of small panel size. We address this by repeating the wild cluster bootstrap analysis at $K \\in \\{3, 5, 7\\}$, constructing all $\\binom{15}{K}$ panels for each value and applying the same specification (Equation~\\ref{eq:regression}) with $K$-position max-$p$ correction.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_regime_transition.pdf}\n\\caption{Regime transition in vulnerability predictors across panel sizes. $\\eta_{\\max}$ significance (orange) declines from 5/12 to 0/12, while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significance (blue) rises from 1/12 to 7/12 as $K$ increases. Shaded region marks the transition zone $K^* \\in (3, 5)$. Bootstrap: $B{=}9999$, Rademacher weights, position-permutation max-$p$, BH FDR $\\alpha{=}0.05$.}\n\\label{fig:regime_transition}\n\\end{figure}\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:regime_transition} reveals a sharp regime transition between $K{=}3$ and $K{=}5$. At $K{=}3$, $\\eta_{\\max}$ is the dominant predictor (5/12) and $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ is negligible (1/12). At $K{=}5$, $\\eta_{\\max}$ drops to 0/12 while $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ rises to 7/12, all positive. Restricting to the four $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$-stable attacks ($\\rho{\\geq}0.91$; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}) yields 4/8 at $K{=}5$, confirming that the signal is not driven by measurement-unstable conditions. The attenuation from 7/12 at $K{=}5$ to 5/12 at $K{=}7$ reflects increased conservatism of the 7-position max-$p$ correction (\\S\\ref{sec:analysis}).\n\n\\subsection{Random vs.\\ Adaptive Fault Contrast}\n\\label{sec:rf_contrast}\n\nWe simulate non-adversarial noise by randomly flipping judge votes at observed per-judge error rates. At $K{=}3$, random faults yield $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$ with $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ significant in 2/2 conditions; under adaptive attack, significance collapses (1/12). At $K{=}5$, random faults yield $R^2 = 0.705$ ($K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ 2/2 significant); adaptive attack reduces $R^2$ to 0.256 but $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ retains predictive power (7/12). The gap narrows from ${\\sim}0.6$ ($K{=}3$) to $0.45$ ($K{=}5$), confirming the contrast is most pronounced in small panels.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_gam_keff_split.pdf}\n\\caption{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ effect direction split by attack type at $K{=}3$ (GAM with FDR correction). Targeted attacks show positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (higher diversity associated with higher vulnerability), while shared-bias attacks show negative $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ (higher diversity associated with lower vulnerability). At $K{=}5$, 11/12 conditions retain this pattern.}\n\\label{fig:gam_split}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Attack-Type Empirical Categorization}\n\\label{sec:gam_taxonomy}\n\nGAM analysis reveals structure masked by the significance collapse (Figure~\\ref{fig:gam_split}). At $K{=}3$, the 12 conditions split 6:6 by attack mechanism. \\textit{Targeted attacks} (prompt injection, score manipulation, position bias) show positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ in all 6 conditions; \\textit{shared-bias attacks} (sycophancy, verbosity bias, authority bias) show negative $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ in all 6. Position bias is an anomalous case within the targeted category: while it shows positive $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ direction, its $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ stability is low ($\\rho{=}0.08$ on ARC; Appendix~\\ref{app:attacked_keff}), placing it closer to a measurement-unstable regime than to the other targeted attacks ($\\rho{\\geq}0.60$). At $K{=}5$, 11/12 conditions maintain the targeted/shared-bias distinction.\n\n\\subsection{Aggregation Rules Modulate the Diversity Channel}\n\\label{sec:aggregation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:aggregation} reports regression results for four aggregation methods across all 12 conditions.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccc}\n\\toprule\n\\textbf{Method} & \\textbf{$\\eta_{\\max}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ sig} & \\textbf{$K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ $\\beta$ dir.} & \\textbf{$R^2$} \\\\\n\\midrule\nThreshold       & 12/12 & 11/12 & 10+/1$-$  & 0.89 \\\\\nMajority        & 9/12  & 8/12  & 7+/1$-$   & 0.298 \\\\\nWeighted        & 9/12  & 9/12  & 9+/0$-$   & 0.284 \\\\\nBayesian        & 9/12  & 7/12  & 1+/6$-$   & 0.104 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\caption{Aggregation method comparison ($K{=}3$, 15 models, 455 panels, log-log canonical spec). Significance counts are FDR-corrected across 12 conditions. $R^2$ is the mean across conditions. Methods are ordered by $R^2$ to highlight the vulnerability gradient from threshold (most predictable) to Bayesian (least).}\n\\label{tab:aggregation}\n\\end{table}\n\n\nThe methods form a vulnerability gradient from threshold ($R^2{=}0.89$) to Bayesian ($R^2{=}0.104$; Appendix~\\ref{app:aggregation_details}). Threshold voting's high $R^2$ is partly structural: unanimity makes panel failure nearly deterministic given the weakest judge. Bayesian calibration is associated with a sign change in the diversity channel: $K_{\\mathrm{eff}}$ direction shifts from predominantly positive (7+/1$-$) to predominantly negative (1+/6$-$).\n",

1. `analysis.tex:8`: "6:6 directional split at K=3 (11/12 consistent at K=5)"
2. `results.tex:70-71` (Figure caption): "Targeted attacks show positive K_eff... shared-bias attacks show negative K_eff. At K=5, 11/12 conditions retain this pattern."
3. `results.tex:77`: "At K=3, the 12 conditions split 6:6 by attack mechanism... 

Targeted attacks show positive K_eff in all 6 conditions; shared-bias attacks show negative K_eff in all 6. At K=5, 11/12 conditions maintain the targeted/shared-bias distinction."
4. `introduction.tex:21`: "Attack-type taxonomy. GAM reveals a 6:6 split by attack mechanism at K=3 (targeted vs. shared-bias), 11/12 consistent at K=5."
5. `introduction.tex:14`: "GAM analysis shows higher K_eff associated with increased vulnerability for targeted attacks but decreased vulnerability for shared-bias attacks"

**Bootstrap GAM results** show something fundamentally different from what the paper claims across all these sections.

With B=9999 bootstrap iterations, both K=3 and K=5 show all 12/12 conditions with positive K_eff and direction positive rates between 0.8895 and 1.0 — there's no sign split at all. The paper's central claim about a 6:6 targeted versus shared-bias distinction is directly contradicted by the bootstrap analysis. What actually emerges is a magnitude difference instead: shared-bias attacks produce smaller slopes (around 0.4 for position bias) while targeted attacks show much larger effects (25-35 for score manipulation), but both point in the same direction.

I'm tracing where the original mixed-sign K=5 result came from — it appears the earlier analysis loaded data differently through a function that returned None, so it may have been working with incorrect or incomplete data. The figure in the paper showing the 6:6 split is based on that original GAM analysis, but I need to verify what the original K=3 analysis actually showed to understand the full picture.

The bootstrap analysis with 9999 resamples is more statistically rigorous and shows all positive effects for K=3, which directly contradicts the original 6:6 split claim. Let me look more carefully at the bootstrap results to understand this discrepancy — the consistency rates are quite high (0.89-1.0), suggesting the positive direction is robust across resamples. The paper claims these should all be negative, but that's the opposite of what I'm finding—every shared-bias condition shows positive slopes with extremely high consistency across 9,999 bootstrap resamples. Even at K=3, the bootstrap's point estimate gives all positive values, yet the original K=3 analysis apparently showed a 6:6 split, which means the two scripts must be loading or processing the data differently.

This is a fundamental problem because the entire Claim 1b rests on that 6:6 split, Figure 2 displays it prominently, and the introduction emphasizes it multiple times as a key finding. But if the more rigorous bootstrap analysis contradicts this, I need to carefully document where the discrepancy originates—whether it's a data loading issue or something else in how the analyses compute their results. Now I need to escalate this as a critical severity issue. The core problem is determining which data source is authoritative—the original analysis or the bootstrap reconstruction—because if the bootstrap is correct, the entire 6:6 split finding collapses and the narrative shifts from a sign disagreement to a magnitude gradient where all conditions show positive K_eff slopes. This requires updates across the introduction, results section, figure captions, and a regeneration of the GAM visualization using the corrected bootstrap data.

sycophancy: direction_positive_rate=0.9998, slope=28.76\n  - verbosity_bias: rate=1.0, slope=14.83\n  - authority_bias: rate=0.9998, slope=36.57\n  - (ARC 侧三个同类条件：rate=1.0)\n- split_consistency_rate = 0.0\n\n**K=5 (3003 panels)**: ALL 12/12 conditions K_eff 方向为正\n- 所有 12 个条件 direction_positive_rate = 1.0（100% bootstrap 一致性）\n- split_consistency_rate = 0.0\n\n**论文当前声明（至少 4 处）**：\n1. `introduction.tex:21`: \"GAM reveals a 6:6 split by attack mechanism at K=3 (targeted vs. shared-bias), 11/12 consistent at K=5\"\n2. `results.tex:77`: \"At K=3, the 12 conditions split 6:6... shared-bias attacks show negative K_eff in all 6\"\n3. `results.tex:70-71` (Fig 2 caption): \"shared-bias attacks show negative K_eff\"\n4. `analysis.tex:8`: \"6:6 directional split at K=3 (11/12 consistent at K=5)\"\n\n## 根因分析\n\nWorker 在 bootstrap 结果中明确指出：原始 `k5_gam_analysis.json` 使用 `load_mi_data() → mi_data[\"mi_matrices\"]` 加载数据，该 key 返回 `None`——说明原始分析可能使用了不同版本的 data loader 或预计算的 K_eff 值。Bootstrap 脚本直接从原始 JSON 加载 MI 矩阵，是 canonical 数据源。\n\n两个脚本 GAM specification 完全一致（我已在上一轮逐行比对过两个脚本），唯一差异是数据来源（K_eff 值不同）。\n\n## 实际模式\n\nBootstrap 揭示的真实模式：K_eff 在所有条件下均为正（更高独立性 → 更高 ASR），但 **幅度有差异**：\n- targeted attacks（prompt_injection, score_manipulation）slope 通常 20-35\n- shared-bias attacks（authority_bias, sycophancy）slope 也是 13-37（正值！）\n- position_bias slope 极小（0.37-0.85）但仍为正\n\n区别是 **magnitude gradient**，不是 **sign split**。\n\n## 影响\n\nClaim 1b 是论文三大贡献之一。如果 bootstrap 是正确的（且 worker 认为它是 canonical source），需要：\n1. 调查数据加载差异的根本原因——确认哪个数据源是正确的\n2. 如果 bootstrap 正确，将\"6:6 sign split\"重新表述为\"magnitude gradient\"\n3. 修改受影响的至少 4 处论文文本 + Figure 2\n4. 重新评估 Contribution 2 的表述强度\n\n## 建议\n\n**优先级最高**：Director 需要立即让 Agent 排查数据加载差异——对比两个脚本实际使用的 K_eff 值（而非 GAM 结果），确定哪个是正确的。在此之前，Claim 1b 不可信。",

```text
This is the aggregation bootstrap analysis. Let me review the key findings:

1. The Bayesian direction reversal (K_eff goes predominantly negative under Bayesian calibration) is confirmed by bootstrap - this is good for the paper's claims about aggregation modulation.

2. The paper's Table 2 numbers need checking against the bootstrap results:
   - Majority: paper says 7+/1-, bootstrap says 11+/1- → discrepancy in positive count
   - Weighted: paper says 9+/0-, bootstrap says 11+/1- → discrepancy
   - Threshold: paper says 10+/1-, bootstrap says 11+/1- → slight discrepancy
   - Bayesian: paper says 1+/6-, bootstrap says 3+/9- → different counts but same reversal pattern

I'm realizing these might be measuring different things - the paper's Table 2 could be reporting significance counts while the bootstrap is showing direction counts regardless of significance. Let me check the actual table from the results file to clarify what each column represents.

Looking at the data, the "K_eff β dir." column shows direction counts only for significant coefficients, while the bootstrap gives direction counts across all conditions tested. For Bayesian specifically, the paper reports 1+/6- out of 7 significant results, but bootstrap shows 3+/9- across all conditions - revealing that Bayesian has a fundamental reversal pattern.

The key insight is that this Bayesian reversal is actually bootstrap-confirmed, with the 6 strongly negative conditions showing near-zero positive rates (0-1.3%), making it a robust finding. This discrepancy between paper and bootstrap isn't a data inconsistency - it's just that the paper only counts significant coefficients while bootstrap counts everything.

Now I'm realizing this might not directly affect my earlier Claim 1b concern since the aggregation results are separate from the 6:6 taxonomy split issue. The Table 2 K_eff directions are from OLS analysis, whereas the taxonomy split comes from GAM analysis, so these are actually measuring different things.

Let me look at the actual aggregation bootstrap data to get more clarity before deciding whether to submit a review.

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Let me analyze the aggregation bootstrap results:

**Key findings:**

1. **Majority vote**: Bootstrap confirms 11+/1- direction (arc_sycophancy is the sole negative). Paper Table 2 says 7+/1- FDR-significant — consistent (the bootstrap counts ALL directions, not just significant ones). ✓

2. **Weighted vote**: Bootstrap confirms 11+/1- (arc_sycophancy weakly negative, 0.432 positive rate). Paper says 9+/0- FDR-significant — consistent. ✓


3. **Threshold**: Bootstrap also confirms 11+/1- with arc_verbosity_bias showing weak negative effects at 0.461 positive rate, matching the paper's 10+/1- FDR-significant results. ✓

4. **Bayesian**: The bootstrap reveals a striking reversal here—9 out of 12 conditions point negative, with 6 showing extremely stable near-zero positive rates below 1.3%. This aligns with the paper's 1+/6- FDR-significant findings. ✓

The bootstrap validation confirms the paper's core claims about the aggregation vulnerability gradient, where threshold methods show the strongest effect and Bayesian approaches show the weakest.

The Bayesian reversal is particularly robust—K_eff shifts predominantly negative under Bayesian calibration. One nuance worth noting: the paper's Table 2 reports only FDR-significant directions while the bootstrap counts all conditions, which explains minor count differences like the threshold showing 11+/1- versus the paper's 10+/1-. Across Majority and Weighted methods, arc_sycophancy consistently emerges as the sole negative condition, and this pattern holds up in the GAM bootstrap results too.

What's striking is the position_bias anomaly in the Bayesian analysis—it shows a 0.944 positive rate while most other conditions flip negative, suggesting this might be a method-specific quirk rather than a fundamental property. The aggregation bootstrap is largely confirmatory overall, so I should post a review noting the main results hold with perhaps a callout about this position_bias behavior in Bayesian.
```

```text
1	% Mirror of abstract in main.tex — do not edit here.
2	
3	Multi-judge LLM evaluation panels are widely assumed to benefit from judge diversity, drawing on fault-tolerance intuitions from distributed systems. We empirically characterize a panel-size-dependent vulnerability regime transition by decomposing panel attack success into individual judge weakness ($\eta_{\max}$) and structural diversity ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$) across 15 models, six attack types, two benchmarks, and panel sizes $K \in \{3, 5, 7\}$ (up to 6,435 panels). At small panels, individual weakness dominates; at larger panels, diversity structure becomes the primary predictor. This aggregate pattern masks attack-mechanism-dependent structure: higher diversity is associated with increased vulnerability for targeted attacks but decreased vulnerability for shared-bias attacks. Aggregation rules further modulate the relationship, with Bayesian calibration corresponding to a sign change in the diversity channel. The defense strategy must match the regime: small panels require screening for individual robustness; larger panels require managing judge independence.
4	
```

</details>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	
3	We document a descriptive regime transition in LLM judge panel vulnerability: individual weakness ($\eta_{\max}$) dominates at $K{=}3$; at $K{\geq}5$, diversity structure ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$) becomes the primary predictor. The aggregate positive $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ association is attack-mechanism-dependent (targeted positive, shared-bias negative); Bayesian calibration corresponds to a sign change at $K{=}3$ and renders both predictors negligible at $K{=}5$. Because $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ are structurally correlated ($r{=}0.70$--$0.81$), the independent causal contribution of diversity remains only partially separable even under capability-matched subsampling (\S\ref{sec:analysis}). For deployment: at $K{=}3$, screen for individual robustness; at $K{\geq}5$, prefer correlated robustness profiles. Causal identification requires training-based diversity control \citep{kariyappa2019improving,pang2019improving}.
4	
```

### Tool result

_source line 4493 · 2026-05-23T17:40:17.376Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01G7wwnsVbS51G5CfduBdd3F`</summary>

```text
65	\label{eq:regression}
66	\log(\mathrm{ASR} + \epsilon) = \alpha + \beta_1 \log(K_{\mathrm{eff}}) + \beta_2 \log(\eta_{\max} + \epsilon),
67	\end{equation}
68	with $\epsilon = 10^{-6}$ to handle zero values (sensitivity to $\epsilon$ is analyzed in Appendix~\ref{app:epsilon}). Standardized coefficients $\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}} = \hat{\beta} \cdot \mathrm{SD}(\log x) / \mathrm{SD}(\log y)$ enable direct comparison of effect magnitudes across predictors and conditions. We apply Benjamini-Hochberg correction for multiple testing, controlling the false discovery rate per predictor across the 12 conditions.
69	
70	To assess relative predictor importance beyond regression coefficients, we perform dominance analysis \citep{budescu1993dominance}. This method decomposes $R^2$ by averaging each predictor's incremental $R^2$ contribution across all possible subsets of predictors. A predictor is \emph{dominant} if its average contribution exceeds that of the competing predictor in every subset ordering. With two predictors, dominance reduces to comparing each predictor's solo $R^2$ with its incremental $R^2$ conditional on the other.
71	
72	To test whether $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s effect direction varies by attack type, we fit generalized additive models (GAMs) with smooth terms for both predictors, allowing nonlinear partial effects. The GAM partial effect of $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is then examined separately for targeted attacks (prompt injection, score manipulation, position bias) and shared-bias attacks (sycophancy, verbosity bias, authority bias).
73	
74	\subsection{Robustness to Non-Independence}
75	\label{sec:robustness_method}
76	
77	The 455 panels share judges combinatorially (each appears in 91 panels), inducing correlated residuals. We correct for this using clustered standard errors (CR1, 3-position max-SE), mixed-effects models (random judge intercepts, 3-position max-$p$), and wild cluster bootstrap ($B{=}9{,}999$, Rademacher weights, 3-position max-$p$) following \citet{cameron2008bootstrap}. The 13 clusters per position are below the 20-cluster threshold recommended by \citet{cameron2008bootstrap}; following \citet{mackinnon2018wild}, we use Rademacher weights, which remain conservative in few-cluster settings even when cluster sizes are unequal. We use the log-log specification rather than fractional response models \citep{papke1996econometric} or beta regression \citep{ferrari2004beta} because the log transform linearizes the multiplicative SDPI structure; fractional logit with clustered SE serves as an $\epsilon$-free robustness check (Appendix~\ref{app:robustness_checks}). Details are in Appendix~\ref{app:robustness_methods}.
78

214	\caption{Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity analysis for $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient across 36 conditions. RV: robustness value (minimum partial $R^2$ an unobserved confounder must have with both $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and ASR to nullify the coefficient). $\eta$ $R^2_{\mathrm{tr}}$/$R^2_{\mathrm{out}}$: partial $R^2$ of $\eta_{\max}$ with $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ (treatment) and ASR (outcome). Exc.: \checkmark\ if RV exceeds $\eta_{\max}$'s partial $R^2$ with treatment, indicating $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s coefficient is robust to the known capability confound. RV exceeds $\eta_{\max}$ in 8/36 conditions ($K{=}3$: 1/12, $K{=}5$: 3/12, $K{=}7$: 4/12).}
215	\label{tab:sensitivity}
216	\end{table}
217	
218	RV ranges from 0.005 to 0.52 across the 36 conditions (median by $K$: 0.30 at $K{=}3$, 0.22 at $K{=}5$, 0.21 at $K{=}7$), revealing heterogeneous robustness: some conditions show $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficients robust to strong confounders (e.g., $K{=}7$ MMLU prompt injection, RV${=}0.52$), while others are fragile (e.g., $K{=}7$ MMLU authority bias, RV${=}0.005$). The known $\eta_{\max}$ confound exceeds RV in 28/36 conditions ($K{=}3$: 11/12, $K{=}5$: 9/12, $K{=}7$: 8/12). The fraction of conditions where $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ is robust to the $\eta_{\max}$ confound increases with panel size (1/12 $\to$ 3/12 $\to$ 4/12), paralleling the regime transition documented in \S\ref{sec:cross_k}.
219	
220	\begin{figure}[h]

223	\caption{Cinelli--Hazlett sensitivity contour plot at $K{=}5$ (representative). $x$-axis: confounder's partial $R^2$ with treatment ($K_{\mathrm{eff}}$); $y$-axis: confounder's partial $R^2$ with outcome (ASR). Contour lines show the adjusted $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient under hypothetical confounders of varying strength. The diamond marks the known $\eta_{\max}$ confound benchmark; conditions where the benchmark falls inside the zero-contour indicate that the $\eta_{\max}$ confound alone could explain away $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s effect.}
224	\label{fig:sensitivity_contour}
225	\end{figure}
226	
227	\section{Cross-Benchmark Prediction Robustness}
228	\label{app:cross_benchmark}
229	
230	To test whether the diversity--vulnerability association reflects structural panel properties rather than benchmark-specific artifacts, we use $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ computed from one benchmark's pairwise MI to predict panel vulnerability on the other. Under wild cluster bootstrap (FDR $\alpha{=}0.05$), MMLU-derived $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ predicts ARC vulnerability in 14/18 conditions across $K \in \{3, 5, 7\}$. The reverse direction (ARC$\to$MMLU) achieves 10/18, with attenuation concentrated at $K{\geq}5$ (4/12), consistent with ARC's binary format producing lower MI discriminability between judge pairs. Cross-benchmark $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ correlations are $r{\approx}0.76$--$0.78$, indicating that $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ primarily captures panel-level compositional structure rather than benchmark-specific judge interactions. The asymmetry between prediction directions further suggests that MI estimated from richer response distributions (MMLU's four-way choice) yields a more transferable diversity measure.
231	
232	\section{Pool A Cinelli--Hazlett Sensitivity}
233	\label{app:sensitivity_poolA}
234	
235	To test whether reducing the $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$--$\eta_{\max}$ collinearity strengthens $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s independent signal, we repeat the Cinelli--Hazlett analysis on the capability-matched Pool~A subsample (9 models, $r{=}0.50$, 84 panels at $K{=}3$, 126 at $K{=}5$). Table~\ref{tab:sensitivity_poolA} compares the full-pool results with Pool~A.

/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/analysis.tex-5-At $K{=}3$, a majority requires $t{=}2$; a single compromised judge flips the panel, making $\eta_{\max}$ the binding constraint. /home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/related_work.tex:16:Classical BFT \citep{lamport1982byzantine,castro1999practical} tolerates up to $f < n/3$ faulty nodes but assumes non-strategic faults; \citet{schneier2000secrets} articulated the weakest-link principle that an attacker exploits the single most vulnerable component. Our framework adopts the Stackelberg structure of \citet{tambe2011security}. Correlated LLM judge errors \citep{kim2025correlated,kuai2026independent} violate BFT's independence assumption, motivating our empirical decomposition.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/related_work.tex-17-
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/related_work.tex-18-\paragraph{Information-Theoretic Evaluation.}
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519_2/projects/cert_manip_resist_eval/docs/paper/related_work.tex-19-Information-theoretic approaches to LLM evaluation address statistical validity \citep{robertson2025stepwise,feng2026noisy}, single-model attack costs \citep{kaneko2025bits}, sequential selection \citep{jung2026logarithmic}, and truthfulness \citep{chen2025incentivizing,qiu2026truthfulness}, but assume honest judges or single-model adversaries. We address the remaining gap: adversarial manipulation interacting with multi-judge composition.
--

The `gam_tier_analysis_corrected.json` is very revealing. Let me extract the GAM keff_sign for all conditions from the original analysis:

From the remote data I can see:
- mmluxprompt_injection: keff_sign = "+"
- mmluxsycophancy: keff_sign = "+" (in canonical OLS)
- mmluxscore_manipulation: keff_sign = "-" (in canonical OLS)
- mmluxverbosity_bias: keff_sign = "+"


- mmluxposition_bias: keff_sign = "+"
- arc_challengexposition_bias: GAM keff_sign = "+"
- arc_challengexauthority_bias: GAM keff_sign = "-" with GCV = 12.06

Wait, that GCV value for arc_challengexauthority_bias is striking—12.06 is much higher than the 5.81 the worker mentioned as the original, and nowhere near the current 0.48. Something's off with how these models are being compared, and I need to dig into what's actually being measured here since the output got truncated before I could see the full picture.

Looking at the bootstrap results, arc_challengexauthority_bias shows a negative keff_sign in the GAM but the bootstrap flagged it as POSITIVE with a slope of 22.07 and perfect direction consistency. That's a real disagreement between the two approaches—the GAM and bootstrap are pointing in opposite directions for this condition.

This actually answers the first question directly: OLS and GAM can absolutely disagree. A GAM smooth can shift direction across different x values, while OLS assumes a single linear trend, so their endpoint-to-endpoint directions might not align. So the real puzzle is that the aggregation bootstrap with majority voting aligns with OLS—both showing arc_sycophancy as negative—while the GAM bootstrap flips it to positive. This suggests the GAM is capturing nonlinear curvature that the linear OLS model misses, and depending on how you measure direction (endpoint-to-endpoint versus average slope), you get opposite conclusions.

But there's a more pressing concern: the original GAM analysis showed a mix of positive and negative conditions matching the paper's 6:6 split, yet the bootstrap GAM shows everything positive. Same method, different data—this points to a version mismatch in the underlying dataset.

Let me work through all four questions with this in mind, starting with how OLS and GAM actually differ in their specifications and what they're measuring. The real issue is that the original GAM found a 6:6 split while the bootstrap version doesn't—that's about the data itself, not the method. I need to identify all the places in the paper that claim this 6:6 split or reference negative K_eff for shared-bias attacks, which spans the abstract, introduction contributions, and results sections including figure captions and the main text around section 5.4.

The key insight is reframing Contribution 2 away from a "sign split" narrative toward a "magnitude gradient" story—all conditions actually show positive K_eff where more diversity increases vulnerability, but targeted attacks just show larger effects than shared-bias ones. This means Figure 2 needs a complete redesign to reflect this corrected interpretation. different amounts." That's less compelling from a policy angle. But the Bayesian reversal holds up under scrutiny, which points to a genuine sign flip. So I could reframe Contribution 2 around the attack-mechanism-dependent magnitude gradient alongside the aggregation-method-dependent sign change. The magnitude gradient is weaker than a sign split—"diversity effect varies in strength" is less surprising than "diversity has opposite effects by attack type"—but the Bayesian reversal is real and still noteworthy since it's the aggregation dimension driving the sign change, not the attack type itself. That's still an interesting finding about how methodology shapes the conclusions.

For the venue target, Contribution 1 stays strong as the main finding, but Contribution 2 needs honest reframing since the magnitude gradient alone probably costs about half a point on novelty. Contribution 3 remains unaffected. The regime transition plus Bayesian reversal plus confound analysis is solid for EMNLP Findings territory, though reaching the main conference gets tougher without that dramatic sign split. Now I'm noticing the K=3 bootstrap worker flagged a data discrepancy—the original GCV was around 5.81 versus the current 0.48—which suggests the underlying score data may have shifted between analyses.

This is critical: if the original 6:6 split was real for that older dataset but the current data doesn't show it, then I need to figure out which version the paper actually uses. The canonical data should be whatever the regression tables are built on. If Table 1's OLS and the bootstrap are using different datasets, that's a consistency problem I need to track down. I should have the Agent investigate whether all analyses—OLS, bootstrap, GAM—are pulling from the same data source.

26	\subsection{BFT Reversal Mechanism: Detailed Evidence}
27	\label{app:bft_reversal}
28	
29	Under standard OLS, the regression results (Table~\ref{tab:dominance}) show $\eta_{\max}$ significant in 9 of 12 conditions and $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ positively associated with ASR in 7. Non-independence correction reveals an asymmetry: $\eta_{\max}$ is significant in 5 to 8 of 12 conditions depending on correction method (clustered SE: 8/12; mixed effects: 7/12; wild cluster bootstrap: 5/12), while $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ collapses to 0--1/12 (clustered SE 0/12; bootstrap 1/12; mixed effects 3/12). The directional pattern (point estimates predominantly positive) persists, but statistical confidence is limited. The random-fault contrast (\S\ref{sec:rf_contrast}) sharpens this: under random faults, $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$'s positive association with panel error survives all corrections (2/2 conditions, $R^2 = 0.73$--$0.81$), indicating a genuine relationship that becomes artifactual under adaptive attack (0--1/12 after correction).
30	
31	Under OLS, the sole negative $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ coefficient is ARC $\times$ sycophancy ($\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{std}} = -0.192$, FDR $p = 0.013$), though this effect does not survive non-independence correction (clustered $p = 0.441$). The GAM taxonomy subsumes this individual exception: the directional pattern across all 6 shared-bias conditions is the stronger evidence, not any single condition's significance.
32	
33	\subsection{Diversity--Capability Tradeoff}
34	\label{app:diversity_capability}
35	
36	The $r = 0.70$--$0.81$ correlation between $K_{\mathrm{eff}}$ and $\eta_{\max}$ ($K{=}3$ to $K{=}5$) reflects a structural feature of the current LLM ecosystem: maximizing panel diversity requires sampling across capability tiers, which systematically introduces less capable and therefore more vulnerable judges. The structural correlation is unaffected by SE correction; it is a property of the model ecosystem, not an artifact of panel overlap. However, the high confound combined with fragile independent significance means that observational regression cannot reliably disentangle diversity's causal contribution from the capability deficit that co-occurs with it.
37	
38	Family-level labels provide indirect evidence that capability proximity matters more than taxonomic grouping: family membership is not a significant predictor of panel vulnerability ($p = 0.179$ on MMLU, $p = 0.385$ on ARC), consistent with models from the same provider exhibiting heterogeneous vulnerability profiles that vary by capability tier rather than by shared architecture \citep{kuncheva2003measures}.
39	
40	\subsection{Aggregation Mechanism Details}
```

1	{
2	  "raw": "诊断完成。核心发现：\n\n**K_eff 值完全一致**，两个脚本从同一 JSON 文件读取相同字段。差异根因是 **ASR 计算的 error handling 不同**：\n\n| | 原始 GAM 脚本 | Bootstrap 脚本 |\n|---|---|---|\n| Error 处理 | `.get(\"winner\", \"tie\")` → error 当作 tie | `_is_valid_score()` → 跳过 error |\n| 结果 | 6:6 split (targeted +, shared-bias -) | 12/12 全部正向 |\n\n**6:6 split 是 bug 产物**。3 个 frontier API 模型 (gemini-3.5-flash 17.7%、gemini-3.1-pro-preview 8.0%、gpt-5.5 3.1%) 有大量 error scores。将 error 当作 \"tie\" 虚增了这些高 K_eff 模型的 ASR，导致 shared-bias attacks 的 GAM partial dependence 被人为翻转为负向。\n\n正确处理 error 后（跳过无效样本），全部 12 个 condition 的 K_eff 效果均为正向。V1 的 shared-bias 负向 slope 仅 -0.15 ~ -2.9，而正确计算的 slope 为 +14 ~ +39，说明原来的\"负向\"极其脆弱。\n\n报告已保存到 `artifacts/diagnostic_gam_keff_mismatch.txt`。"
3	}

_source line 4527 · 2026-05-23T17:47:17.764Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
This is a very important finding. The root cause has been identified:

**The 6:6 split was a BUG, not a real finding.** 

The difference between the two scripts was NOT the K_eff values (which are identical) but the **ASR calculation**:
- Original GAM script: `.get("winner", "tie")` → treats errors as ties, inflating ASR for frontier API models (gemini-3.5-flash 17.7% errors, gemini-3.1-pro-preview 8.0%, gpt-5.5 3.1%)


- Bootstrap script: skips errors entirely, computing ASR only from valid samples

Treating errors as ties artificially inflated ASR for high-K_eff models, which asymmetrically skewed the GAM partial dependence for shared-bias attacks negative. The bootstrap's approach of excluding invalid scores is correct, making the 12/12 positive result the true finding, with corrected slopes now showing substantial positive effects (+14 to +) compared to the original tiny negative ones (-0.15 to -2.9).

The 6:6 split was entirely a data processing artifact, confirming my P0 recommendation was justified. I need to review the diagnostic report and post findings that the paper must be revised to remove the 6:6 claim.
```

1	GAM K_eff 数据加载差异诊断报告
2	================================
3	日期: 2026-05-23
4	
5	1. 脚本路径与 K_eff 加载方式
6	─────────────────────────────
7	
8	原始 GAM 脚本:
9	  路径: /root/cert_manip_resist_eval/scripts/run_gam_tier_analysis.py
10	  K_eff 加载: mi_data["keff_per_panel"][ds][pk]["keff"]
11	  来源: load_mi_data() → artifacts/results/plan001/mi_matrix/mi_matrix_15models.json
12	  公式: empirical_keff_from_rho (全对 copula ρ 矩阵)
13	
14	Bootstrap 脚本:
15	  路径: /root/cert_manip_resist_eval/scripts/run_gam_bootstrap_ci_k3.py
16	  K_eff 加载: mi_data["keff_per_panel"][ds][pk]["keff"]  (完全相同)
17	  来源: 同上
18	
19	结论: K_eff 值 **完全一致**，两个脚本读取相同的 JSON 文件相同的字段。
20	
21	Corrected 版本:
22	  路径: /root/cert_manip_resist_eval/scripts/run_gam_tier_analysis_corrected.py
23	  仅修改了 tier 分类 (4/6/5 → 3/3/9)，未修复 ASR 计算的 error handling
24	
25	2. 差异根因: ASR 计算的 Error Handling 不同
26	──────────────────────────────────────────────
27	
28	原始 GAM 脚本:
29	  clean_list[i].get("winner", "tie")    # error → 当作 "tie"
30	  attacked_list[i].get("winner", "tie") # error → 当作 "tie"
31	
32	Bootstrap 脚本:
33	  _is_valid_score(sc)  # error → 跳过该样本
34	  要求 sc["winner"] is not None 且 sc.get("error") 为 falsy
35	
36	影响: 当 API 模型返回 error 时:
37	  - 原始脚本: error 被当作 "tie" 投票，可能被误计为 flip → ASR 被虚增
38	  - Bootstrap 脚本: error 样本被跳过 → ASR 基于有效样本计算

65	arc_challengexprompt_injection        +            +          OK    targeted
66	arc_challengexscore_manipulation      +            +          OK    targeted
67	arc_challengexposition_bias           +            +          OK    targeted
68	arc_challengexsycophancy              -            +          DIFF  shared-bias
69	arc_challengexverbosity_bias          -            +          DIFF  shared-bias
70	arc_challengexauthority_bias          -            +          DIFF  shared-bias
71	
72	6 个 DIFF 全部是 shared-bias attacks，6 个 OK 全部是 targeted attacks。
73	
74	5. 因果链分析
75	─────────────
76	
77	(a) gemini-3.5-flash, gemini-3.1-pro-preview, gpt-5.5 大量 error scores
78	(b) 原始脚本将 error 当作 "tie" → 这些 frontier 模型的 individual ASR 被虚增
79	    例: gemini-3.5-flash sycophancy V1=0.0794 vs V2=0.0000 (完全是 error 伪造的 ASR)
80	(c) 含这些 frontier 模型的 panel: η_max 和 panel_ASR 同时被抬高
81	(d) Frontier 模型通常有较高 K_eff (因为能力差异大, 判决独立性高)
82	(e) 错误虚增了高 K_eff panels 的 ASR → partial dependence 负向
83	(f) 这种虚增仅对 shared-bias attacks 达到翻转方向的程度,
84	    因为 targeted attacks 本身 ASR 足够高, error 的相对影响较小
85	
86	6. 结论
87	────────
88	
89	K_eff 值一致。差异来自 ASR 计算: 原始脚本将 error scores 当作 "tie"
90	(错误做法), bootstrap 脚本跳过 error scores (正确做法)。
91	
92	**6:6 split 是 error handling bug 的产物**, 在正确处理 error 后不成立。
93	正确的结果是 12/12 全部正向: K_eff 越高, panel ASR 越高 (无论攻击类型)。
94	
95	原始 GAM 脚本中 V1 shared-bias 的 GAM slope 量级很小 (-0.15 ~ -2.9),
96	而 V2 的 slope 量级为 14~39, 说明 V1 的 "负向" 效果本身就微弱,
97	仅仅因为 ~3% 的 error scores 就翻转了方向。
98	
99	7. 建议修复
100	───────────
101	
102	将 run_gam_tier_analysis.py 和 run_gam_tier_analysis_corrected.py 中的
103	ASR 计算改为使用 _is_valid_score() 过滤 error scores, 与 bootstrap 脚本
104	保持一致。修复后需要重新跑一遍, 并更新论文中关于 6:6 split 的描述。
105	
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
This is an extremely clear and thorough diagnostic. The root cause is now definitively identified:

1. **K_eff values are IDENTICAL** between the two scripts — same JSON file, same field
2. **The difference is in ASR calculation**: original script treats errors as "tie" (wrong), bootstrap script skips errors (correct)
3. **Three frontier API models** (gemini-3.5-flash 17.7%, gemini-3.1-pro-preview 8.0%, gpt-5.5 3.1%) had high error rates
4. **The causal chain**: errors → treated as ties → frontier models' ASR inflated → frontier models have high K_eff → high-K_eff panels' ASR inflated → GAM partial dependence for shared-bias flipped negative


5. **The original negative slopes were negligible** (-0.15 to -2.9) compared to the corrected positive slopes (+14 to +39), so the "negative" effect was essentially noise
6. **All 6 DIFF conditions are shared-bias, all 6 OK conditions are targeted** — this perfect 6:6 split was entirely an artifact of the bug

This is a textbook example of a data processing error creating a spurious finding. The 6:6 split was never real. Now I need to post a review confirming the root cause and its implications, and also check whether this same error handling bug affected the Table 1 OLS results.

The key question is whether Table 1 used the same buggy error handling as the original GAM script. If it did, the OLS numbers could be wrong, though OLS is less sensitive to a few erroneous data points than GAM is. But the worker mentioned the main ASR computation scripts likely use a different pipeline, and Table 1 might use yet another data loading path entirely. I need to clarify which pipeline generated the data for Table 1, but the priority right now is confirming this finding about the GAM bug. I need to verify that the wild cluster bootstrap analysis—which shows the effective cluster counts at different K values—relied on the correct error handling rather than the buggy version, since that's what validates the regime transition findings. Let me post the review and flag this additional concern about whether the OLS and regime transition analyses used the proper error handling.

2. Wild cluster bootstrap K=3 (run_wild_cluster_bootstrap.py) — confirmed
3. Wild cluster bootstrap v2 (run_wild_cluster_bootstrap_v2.py) — confirmed
4. Wild cluster bootstrap K=5 (run_k5_wild_cluster_bootstrap.py) — confirmed


5. Aggregation bootstrap K=3 (run_k3_aggregation_bootstrap.py) — confirmed

Only the GAM bootstrap scripts use the correct filtering. This means the paper's core quantitative results are potentially compromised across multiple tables and figures.

Actually, wait — I need to reconsider the aggregation bootstrap. It also uses the same buggy handling as the other scripts, so my earlier confirmation was misleading. Both the aggregation bootstrap and Table 2 are wrong in the same way, which made them appear consistent. The worker's analysis suggests the bug's impact on shared-bias attacks' GAM directions depends on the error rate calculation, but I'm realizing the scope of the problem is even broader than initially assessed. I need to escalate this finding about the error handling issue and get the core analyses rerun to confirm the regime transition pattern holds despite the ASR inflation.

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
This is a very thorough audit. The key findings:

1. **Table 1**: AFFECTED by V1 bug. Both `run_condition_dominance.py` and `run_residualized_keff_15model.py` use V1.
2. **Table 2**: PARTIALLY affected. The log-log version uses V1, but the residualized version uses V2. Need to confirm which version the paper uses.
3. **Regime Transition (Figure 3)**: AFFECTED. All three bootstrap scripts (K=3, K=5, K=7) use V1.


4. The error rates for gemini-3.5-flash are concerning at 25-32% on MMLU, which could amplify the bug's impact beyond just the GAM directions. There's a critical detail about how errors treated as ties might systematically distort the ASR measurements — if an error becomes a tie while the clean version remains correct, this could artificially inflate the attack success rate rather than suppress it.

The real issue is that errors create spurious flips: when the clean version has an error (counted as a tie) but the attacked version produces a valid response, or vice versa, it registers as a flip even though no actual attack effect occurred. This is exactly what happened with gemini-3.5-flash on sycophancy — the V1 ASR of 0.0794 versus V2's 0.0000 suggests the entire measured effect was an artifact of error handling rather than genuine attack success.

Now I need to post a follow-up review that acknowledges this worker's audit confirms my earlier concerns while highlighting the critical new details: the script-by-script breakdown and specific error rate data that make the case for immediate action even stronger. The fix already exists in the unified_data_loader but hasn't been applied to the analysis scripts yet, so this is a deployment problem rather than a code problem.
```

1	{
2	  "raw": "诊断完成。\n\n---\n\n## 诊断报告\n\n### 1. 原始 GAM 脚本\n\n**路径**: `/root/cert_manip_resist_eval/scripts/run_gam_tier_analysis.py` (May 23 03:08)\n\n**数据加载**: `load_mi_data()` → 统一 MI 矩阵 (`mi_matrix_15models.json`)\n\n**ASR 计算**: 定义自己的 `compute_individual_asr()`，用 `.get(\"winner\", \"tie\")` 处理无效分数 → error 响应被当作 \"tie\" 投票\n\n**GAM 规格**: `LinearGAM(s(0, n_splines=8) + s(1, n_splines=8))`，用 `gam.gridsearch()` 自动选 lambda\n\n**符号定义**: `pdep[-1] > pdep[0]` on `generate_X_grid(term=0, n=100)` → 与 bootstrap 完全相同\n\n### 2. Bootstrap 脚本\n\n**路径**: `/root/cert_manip_resist_eval/scripts/run_gam_bootstrap_ci_k3.py` (May 24 01:10)\n\n**数据加载**: 同一个 `load_mi_data()` → 同一个 MI 矩阵\n\n**ASR 计算**: 从 `unified_data_loader` 导入 `_is_valid_score`，**过滤掉 error/无效分数**，不计入 ASR\n\n**GAM 规格**: 完全相同的 `LinearGAM(s(0, n_splines=8))`，gridsearch 选 lambda 后固定给 bootstrap 样本\n\n### 3. K_eff 对比结果\n\n**K_eff 值完全一致** — 两个脚本使用同一个 `load_mi_data()` 读同一个 `mi_matrix_15models.json`，不存在版本差异。\n\n差异来源是 **ASR (y 变量)**，不是 K_eff (x 变量)。\n\n### 4. 根因：ASR 计算 bug + gridsearch 随机性\n\nPer-condition sign 对比：\n\n| Condition | 原始 GAM | Bootstrap | 匹配 |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| mmlu×prompt_injection | + | + | OK |\n| mmlu×score_manipulation | + | + | OK |\n| mmlu×position_bias | + | + | OK |\n| **mmlu×sycophancy** | **-** | **+** | MISMATCH |\n| **mmlu×verbosity_bias** | **-** | **+** | MISMATCH |\n| **mmlu×authority_bias** | **-** | **+** | MISMATCH |\n| arc×prompt_injection | + | + | OK |\n| arc×score_manipulation | + | + | OK |\n| arc×position_bias | + | + | OK |\n| **arc×sycophancy** | **-** | **+** | MISMATCH |\n| **arc×verbosity_bias** | **-** | **+** | MISMATCH |\n| **arc×authority_bias** | **-** | **+** | MISMATCH |\n\n6 个 MISMATCH **全部是 shared_bias 攻击**（sycophancy/verbosity_bias/authority_bias），完美对应论文中声称的 taxonomy split。\n\n**Bug 机制**：\n- gpt-5.5、gemini-3.1-pro-preview、gemini-3.5-flash 有 ~4%（MMLU）和 ~1%（ARC）的 error 分数\n- 原始脚本将 error 当 \"tie\"，产生虚假 ASR（如 gemini-3.1-pro-preview mmlu×sycophancy: 原始=0.0474, 过滤后=0.0000）\n- 对 shared_bias 攻击（真实 ASR 低），这些虚假翻转按比例影响更大\n- 膨胀的 frontier ASR 改变了 K_eff→ASR 的 GAM partial dependence 曲面形状\n- 加上 `gridsearch()` 的 lambda 随机性（我的 fresh run 用原始方法只复现 2/6 负号而非 6/6），共同产生了 6:6 split\n\n### 5. Table 1 OLS 使用的版本\n\n**脚本**: `run_plan001_step4_panel_regression.py`\n\n**导入**: `compute_corrected_asr, compute_corrected_panel_asr` → 使用 **`_is_valid_score` 过滤**（与 bootstrap 一致）\n\n**结果**: 12/12 条件 keff_beta **全部正向**，全部显著（p < 0.02）\n\nTable 1 OLS 使用的是 **正确版本** 的数据。\n\n### 5. 结论\n\n| 脚本 | ASR 方法 | K_eff sign 结果 |\n|---|---|---|\n| `run_gam_tier_analysis.py` | 旧（error→tie） | 6:6 split |\n| `run_gam_tier_analysis_corrected.py` | 旧（error→tie） | 6:6 split |\n| `run_gam_bootstrap_ci_k3.py` | 新（过滤 error） | 12/12 positive |\n| `run_plan001_step4_panel_regression.py` | 新（过滤 error） | 12/12 positive |\n\n**Canonical 数据源**: 过滤版 ASR（`_is_valid_score`），被 Table 1 OLS 和 bootstrap 使用。\n\n**6:6 split 产生者**: `run_gam_tier_analysis.py` 中的旧 ASR 计算（将 error 当 tie）+ pygam gridsearch 随机性。这是 artifact，不是 genuine pattern。论文 Section 4.4 的 taxonomy split 声明需要修正。"

- K_eff negative: 1 (ARC×sycophancy) → **After: 0** (all positive)
- Dominance: Co:7, η:4, K:1 → **After: Co:10, K:2, η:0**


- K_eff is now statistically significant across all 12 conditions at K=3, jumping from just 8 out of 12, and there are no longer any purely η-dominant conditions. This fundamentally reshapes the paper's narrative around regime transitions—the current story about η_max dominance at K=3 needs substantial revision since K_eff is now co-dominant or dominant everywhere.

The real question is whether this holds up under wild cluster bootstrap correction with the updated ASR method. If K_eff significance drops back to around 5 out of 12 after accounting for panel overlap, the transition becomes much less dramatic—moving from 5/12 to 7/12 rather than the stark 1/12 to 7/12 shift currently claimed. The five conditions that flipped are primarily...

Looking at the corrected values now, the MMLU × Score interaction shows the most striking reversal: η plummets from 0.60 to 0.08 while K jumps from 0.03 to 0.58, completely flipping which effect dominates. The ARC × Sycophancy coefficient also flips sign from -0.19 to +0.26, which aligns with what the GAM bootstrap suggested. I need to check the full corrected JSON to see the complete picture before summarizing.

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
This is a massive finding. Let me summarize the impact:

**Corrected Table 1 (V2 ASR):**
- η_max significant: 11/12 → 10/12 (slight decrease)
- K_eff significant: 8/12 → **12/12** (ALL conditions!)
- K_eff all positive (was 1 negative)
- Dominance: {Co:7, η:4, K:1} → {**Co:10, K:2, η:0**}


- **No pure η-dominant conditions remain**

This completely reframes the paper's story. The original narrative claimed η_max dominated at K=3 across nearly all conditions while K_eff was weak, but the corrected results show K_eff is actually significant everywhere and co-dominant in most cases, with zero conditions where η alone drives the effect. The critical next step is checking whether this pattern holds up after applying wild cluster bootstrap corrections.

I'm noticing a major contradiction in the interpretation though. The worker claims "diversity is protective across all conditions," but K_eff is universally positive, meaning higher effective diversity correlates with higher ASR—more vulnerability, not less. A protective effect would require negative coefficients, so the language here is backwards.

Looking at the coefficient changes, there's a clear pattern: η_max's standardized coefficients are shrinking dramatically while K_eff's are growing. This tracks because the bug artificially inflated ASR for high-η models, creating a spurious correlation that masked K_eff's true relationship with vulnerability. Once those errors are corrected, the artificial signal disappears and K_eff emerges as the stronger predictor.

The critical issue for the paper's narrative is whether the K=3 bootstrap results would shift if rerun with corrected ASR—K_eff could jump from barely significant to moderately strong, which would undermine the claimed sharp regime transition between K=3 and K=5.

Even if K_eff strengthens at K=3, the transition might persist as a more gradual shift rather than a dramatic threshold effect, and the η_max pattern could remain largely unchanged. Either way, Table 1 needs updating and the bootstrap analysis requires rerunning, which could substantially reshape the paper's core argument.
```

  "text": "[severity: critical] 修正后 Table 1 结果——K_eff 从 8/12 升至 12/12 全显著，论文叙事需要重大调整\n\n## 修正数据核实\n\nWorker 用 V2 correct ASR 重跑了 K=3 OLS。核心变化：\n\n| 指标 | 旧（V1 buggy） | 新（V2 correct） |\n|------|---------------|------------------|\n| η_max 显著 | 11/12 | 10/12 |\n| K_eff 显著 | 8/12 | **12/12** |\n| K_eff 负向 | 1（ARC×sycophancy） | **0**（全部正向） |\n| Dominance: Co | 7 | **10** |\n| Dominance: η sole | 4 | **0** |\n| Dominance: K sole | 1 | 2 |\n\n5 个条件发生了 significance/dominance 变化，其中最戏剧性的是 MMLU×Score manipulation 从 η-dominant 直接翻转为 K-dominant（η β_std 从 0.60 跌至 0.08，K β_std 从 0.03 升至 0.58）。\n\n### 系统性模式\n\n修正后 η_max β_std **全线下降**，K_eff β_std **全线上升**：\n- mmlu_prompt_injection: η 0.62→0.18, K 0.25→0.51\n- mmlu_sycophancy: η 0.49→0.19, K 0.17→0.48\n- mmlu_authority_bias: η 0.38→0.10, K 0.01→0.50\n\n**机制**：bug 将 API error 当作 tie → 虚增了高 error 率 frontier 模型的 ASR → 这些模型同时是高 η_max 模型 → η_max 与 ASR 的人为相关被夸大 → η_max 看起来更重要，K_eff 被掩盖。\n\n### 对论文叙事的影响\n\n**Table 1 现状（论文中）**：\"η_max reaches FDR significance in 9/12 conditions... K_eff is significant positive in 7/12 and negative in 1\"\n\n**修正后**：K_eff 12/12 全显著全正向，Co-dominant 10/12。**没有任何 η-dominant 条件**。\n\n这对 Contribution 1（Regime Transition）有重大影响：\n- 论文说 \"At K=3, η_max dominates, K_eff is negligible (1/12 under bootstrap)\"\n- 但修正后的 OLS 显示 K_eff 在 K=3 就已经 12/12 显著、强效应\n- 如果 V2 bootstrap 也显示 K_eff 在 K=3 有更多显著条件（比如从 1/12 升到 4-5/12），\"sharp transition\" 会变成 \"gradual transition\"\n\n### 仍需验证\n\n**最关键的缺失**：V2 correct ASR 的 K=3/5/7 wild cluster bootstrap 尚未重跑。OLS 的 12/12 会被 bootstrap 压缩（校正 panel 非独立性），但压缩到什么程度决定了 regime transition 叙事能否存活。\n\n如果 V2 bootstrap K=3 结果是：\n- K_eff 1-2/12 → regime transition 基本不变，只需更新 Table 1\n- K_eff 3-5/12 → transition 变得 gradual，叙事需要调整但仍有 pattern\n- K_eff 6+/12 → transition 不存在，Contribution 1 需要根本重构\n\n**建议**：V2 ASR K=3/5/7 wild cluster bootstrap 是当前**最高优先级**任务。所有论文修改必须等这个结果。\n\n### 小问题\n\nWorker 结论说 \"Diversity is protective across ALL conditions\"——这是错误解读。所有 K_eff β 都是**正向**的，意味着更高 K_eff → 更高 ASR → 多样性**增加**脆弱性，不是 protective。方向没变（始终为正），只是效应更强了。",
  "project": "cert_manip_resist_eval"
}

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
This is the critical result I was waiting for. Let me analyze:

**Regime Transition after V2 correction:**

| K | η_max sig (old → new) | K_eff sig (old → new) |
|---|---|---|
| K=3 | 5 → 3 | 1 → **4** |
| K=5 | 0 → 0 | 7 → **10** |
| K=7 | 0 → 0 | 5 → **9** |

**Impact assessment:**

1. **Regime transition still EXISTS** but is less sharp:
   - Old: K_eff 1/12 → 7/12 → 5/12 (dramatic jump from K=3 to K=5)
   - New: K_eff 4/12 → 10/12 → 9/12 (still increases, but starts from a higher base)

2. **The crossover still exists**: At K=3, η_max (3/12) is close to K_eff (4/12) — they're roughly equal. At K=5, K_eff (10/12) completely dominates η_max (0/12). So there IS still a transition, but it's shifted from a sharp boundary to a more gradual regime where K_eff takes over as the dominant signal.

3. **The narrative changes from "sharp transition" to "monotonic strengthening":** K_eff's significance increases steadily with K (4→10→9), while η_max decreases (3→0→0). At K=3 both contribute meaningfully; by K≥5, K_eff becomes the sole significant predictor.

4. **The K_eff attenuation from K=5 to K=7** is now much less dramatic (10→9 instead of 7→5), suggesting the mechanism driving the decline at higher K values has changed.

5. **The "informative null" claim needs revision:** Since K_eff is now 4/12 significant at K=3, it's no longer truly an informative null—there's actual signal present rather than just a meaningful absence of effect.

6. **Figure 3 needs redrawing** to reflect a less dramatic but still observable separation between the regimes.

For Contribution 1, the regime transition is weaker but still holds: η_max significance decreases monotonically (3→0→0), K_eff significance increases (4→10→9), and the transition point remains around K≥5.

The key changes are that K_eff shows meaningful signal even at K=3 (4/12 instead of negligible), and the crossover is more gradual—a 4→10 progression rather than a sharp 1→7 jump. The core finding that η_max fades while K_eff strengthens is still novel, just less dramatic than initially claimed. I should check the corrected JSON to see the full picture.
```

Looking at the per-condition breakdown at K=3, I'm seeing K_eff significance concentrated in a few specific conditions like sycophancy and verbosity bias, while η_max shows significance in overlapping but distinct conditions—this pattern might be worth highlighting to show how the two metrics carve up the effect space differently.
